`VbaSourceAdmissionIntent.Build`; the separate materialization intent is
`WorkbookMaterializationIntent.SourceSnapshotBuild`. Explicit import uses
`VbaSourceAdmissionIntent.ExplicitImport`; its distinct materialization intent
is `WorkbookMaterializationIntent.ExplicitImport`. The mirror consumes
immutable admitted Unicode, fixed ACP, identities, syntax, provenance, and
captured sidecars without calling `GetACP`, redetecting source encoding, or
rereading caller files. DAP encoding validation remains adapter-owned and is
not accepted as proof by VbaDev. Snapshot Build and Test feature versions are
`2.0`.

Publish checks case-insensitive flat filename collisions across all candidates
before content reads or filtering. Manifest test-only sources and sidecars are
excluded unread, while other installed CommonModules ignore source markers.
Each project-local candidate strict-decodes and reproduces its complete bytes
before the existing first-32-physical-lines, VBA-leading-whitespace-trimmed,
case-insensitive `'#ExcludePublish` prefix decision. A proved exclusion bypasses
declared-kind, identity, syntax-derived import eligibility, ACP projection, and
sidecar capture. Invalid bytes cannot be excused by an earlier marker, but
excluded supported-BOM Unicode need not be representable in ACP. Publish retains
manifest order for included CommonModules followed by remaining filenames,
orphaned entries, absent-entry treatment, and an accepted empty effective set.
Included source facts and captured sidecars remain authoritative through
preflight and verification even if authoring files subsequently change. No
retry, reinventory, closing stability fence, or authoring lock is introduced.

Every included `.bas`, `.cls`, and `.frm` must strict-decode and re-encode to its original bytes, then strict-encode the resulting Unicode text into the operation ACP and decode the staged bytes again to require exact text equality. An unsupported encoding, unrepresentable character, best-fit substitution, or other difference fails before Excel starts. `.frx` sidecars are copied byte-for-byte beside the staged `.frm` under the same base name; explicit import, ordinary Build, Publish, and snapshot Build/Test use the sidecar bytes captured by admission. Source or snapshot bytes are never rewritten, and the command removes only its staged mirror with invocation scratch. Source already in the operation ACP produces the same text bytes, while supported Unicode source is deliberately normalized only at this private VBE boundary. The data-only `fixtures/vba-source-encoding/cases.json` corpus covers ACP 932, 1252, and 65001 through product-local loaders without sharing product test implementations.

Before import, `VbaDev` derives `VbaCodeModuleProjection` from the strict-decoded Unicode source. After each component import and before workbook save, it uses that projection to exactly verify the imported component name, kind, line count, and every projected `CodeModule` line. Export-only class `VERSION` and `BEGIN`/`END` headers, `Attribute` records, UserForm designer records, and the synthetic physical line representing only a terminal newline are excluded; the known leading empty line produced by UserForm import is included. The contract assumes no automatic VBE insertion or normalization beyond this projection. Any unmodeled difference fails before save, preventing generated-output commit or explicit-import target persistence.

That runtime proof intentionally ends at component identity, kind, and projected code. `VbaDev` does not re-export each component, inspect a non-exhaustive subset of COM-visible properties, or claim per-command verification of export-only metadata, UserForm designer state, or `.frx` content. `VBComponents.Import` is authoritative for that state within a supported Excel environment. Representative real-Excel integration fixtures exercise class and member attributes and sidecar-backed UserForm controls and properties through import, save, close, and reopen. They detect regressions in the supported import path rather than proving every arbitrary input at runtime. Their oracle is semantic: it compares expected attributes, control structure, selected properties, and readable binary sidecar-backed values immediately after import and after reopen. It does not require whole re-exported text or `.frx` bytes to remain identical after VBE record reordering, default materialization, or equivalent reserialization.

Ordinary commands do not close and reopen the generated workbook solely to repeat component or projected-code verification after save. A save failure fails the command and prevents atomic output replacement or persistence of an explicit-import target. Save/close/reopen fidelity remains a release-blocking real-Excel integration fixture, avoiding a second workbook-open lifecycle, its events and prompts, and another open deadline on every build, publish, test build, and import.

The minimum fixture set includes valid non-default class and member attributes, non-ASCII text chosen to strict-round-trip through the host's active ACP, and a UserForm built only from Office-provided intrinsic controls. The form contains a nested `Frame` with child `Label` and `TextBox` controls and an `Image` or equivalent `.frx`-backed value. The semantic oracle checks names, kinds, parent-child relationships, selected stable properties, and successful reading of the binary value immediately after import and after reopen. Third-party ActiveX controls are outside the baseline.

The fixture is part of the existing `WindowsExcelIntegration` category, reached by `npm run test:windows-excel-integration` and the required `npm run verify:release:windows-excel` path. It is not added to ordinary unit or pull-request test runs that cannot assume an installed, licensed Excel/VBE host.

Runtime import does not restrict the active ANSI code page to 932 or another allowlist. It accepts the operation's `GetACP` value when .NET can create its strict encoding and all source passes the required byte and Unicode round trips, followed by imported-component and projected-code verification. Excel 16.0 / ACP 932 is the initial release-blocking real-Excel baseline. Deterministic tests without Excel cover code-page selection, strict conversion, and ACP 65001 canonicalization for 932, 1252, and 65001. Running the same semantic fixture on another real-Excel host adds that environment to the tested baseline; absence from the empirical matrix does not itself reject an ACP at runtime. Integration results record Excel version and active ACP.

Every Excel-automating command invocation strongly owns every dedicated **AutomationExcelProcess** it starts for as long as that process remains live. One invocation may start zero, one, or multiple such processes sequentially: ordinary and snapshot `test` may close a build process before starting a fresh test-execution process, while `test --no-build` needs only the execution process. No process is shared across invocations or with a user's Excel session. Every process is created suspended on a unique invocation-scoped private Windows desktop with atomic Job ownership, observed by exact PID before primary-thread resume, bound through explicit enumeration of that desktop, and retained with the desktop until the complete Job process tree exits. The shared lifecycle has no command switch, best-effort mode, delayed-hiding substitute, or caller-desktop fallback. An unexpected prompt remains private and becomes a bounded failure with available PID, HWND, desktop, class, title, and lifecycle-phase evidence. This contract applies to `new excel`, `build`, every `test` mode, `publish`, `export`, `import`, `host-event list`, `reference add` and either `reference list` mode while they perform a VBE-equivalent ambiguity probe, `common-module add` or `common-module update` while resolving an ambiguous missing required reference, and active `vba-dev doctor` probes. Registry-only reference resolution, `vba-dev check`, a CommonModules mutation that needs no ambiguity probe, `reference remove`, completion, help, and capability inspection do not start Excel. Cancellation first requests cooperative cleanup of every live owned process. For commands other than `new excel`, CommonModules Add, CommonModules Update, and `host-event list`, failure to finish within a bounded grace period permits force termination of the command and its owned Excel process trees. The VS Code caller exempts CommonModules Add and Update from that client-side command force-kill timer because it cannot observe **CommonModulesMutationCommitmentBoundary** safely, exempts `new excel` because it cannot observe the initial manifest success boundary or prove rollback, and exempts `host-event list` because replacement must not start before the CLI has released its owned Excel process. In each case it sends cooperative cancellation and waits for the CLI to exit. During CommonModules preflight, throughout `new excel`, and during Host Event catalog cleanup, the CLI still applies the ordinary bounded cleanup and force termination to any owned Excel process. `new excel` then completes project-artifact rollback and lease-result classification before returning `130` or failure; CommonModules instead defers cancellation after source mutation through commit or recovery-result determination; `host-event list` returns only after its inspection workspace and owned-process release have reached their terminal classification. Extension shutdown, terminal or operating-system process loss, or another abrupt termination remains the crash-recovery case rather than cooperative cancellation. Cleanup removes incomplete temporary outputs while preserving previously completed bin and publish outputs, and it never terminates or reuses a user's existing Excel process.

When an integrating caller has verified `featureVersions["invocation.stdinCancellation"] == "1.0"`, it may start an ordinary `vba-dev` command with the hidden `--cancellation-transport stdin-v1` option. The capability is executable-wide and is not repeated in command entries. In that mode the caller requests cooperative cancellation by writing exactly the BOM-less UTF-8 bytes `cancel\n` with LF only, and the CLI idempotently links receipt to the command action's cancellation token. Repeated valid frames retain the same one-time effect. Standard-input EOF, `cancel` without LF, CRLF, a BOM, and unknown, incomplete, or oversized input are boundedly discarded, are not cancellation requests, and do not create a project-command failure or result-stream diagnostic. EOF in particular never establishes exit `130`, because caller shutdown or loss can close the same anonymous pipe. The extension sends its single request with `stdin.end("cancel\n")`, adds no acknowledgement protocol, and treats write completion only as a transport operation rather than proof that cancellation won. `EPIPE` or another write failure does not set terminal cancellation state. While the command remains live, the extension writes the transport error to VBA Tools Output and changes progress to `Cancellation request could not be delivered; waiting for vba-dev to finish.` without a separate popup. The extension accumulates output until the Node.js child `close` event rather than resolving on `exit`, ensuring stdout and stderr have closed before it validates JSON, cleanup diagnostics, and the terminal code. Exit `130` remains silent; a failure or untrusted result uses its existing single error notification. A trusted success with a cancellation-write failure uses the command's one warning notification, appends `Cancellation request could not be delivered.`, and offers Show Output without changing the CLI warning array or its count. The feature governs request delivery only: the caller waits for the command's authoritative transaction boundary, terminal exit, and trusted result rather than replacing them with its local request state. The extension uses it for every managed ordinary command, while the no-force-kill policy remains specific to `new excel`, CommonModules Add, CommonModules Update, and `host-event list`; those commands remain awaited even after a cancellation-write failure. Without the option the CLI does not consume standard input and direct terminal Ctrl+C remains the cancellation path. The debug adapter does not use this transport because DAP owns its standard input.

Non-debug Excel automation has finite default timeouts and reports timeout as a distinct command outcome before entering the same two-stage cancellation path. Long-running workbook tests may extend only their macro execution deadline through `vba-dev test --timeout-seconds <positive-whole-seconds>` or `commandDefaults.test.executionTimeoutSeconds` in the project manifest. Interactive debug workbook-open and VBE compile prompts remain unbounded because the user must be able to answer or cancel them explicitly.

Timeouts are stage deadlines rather than one wall-clock deadline for the complete command. Excel process start, workbook open or creation, empty UserForm creation, Host Event inspection, each reference-normalization attempt, module import or export, workbook save, test macro execution, and process close report their own stage identity and timeout outcome. Reference-candidate, module, and test counts may increase total command duration without consuming one shared deadline.

Excel process start, Host Event workbook creation, and empty UserForm creation each default to 30 seconds; Host Event inspection defaults to 60 seconds; each `References.AddFromGuid` attempt and returned-identity inspection defaults to 60 seconds; each module import or export defaults to 30 seconds; and test macro execution defaults to 600 seconds. Reference-normalization and per-module deadlines restart for each attempt or module so a larger candidate or source set may take proportionally longer without weakening stuck-operation detection. Cooperative process cleanup has a 5-second grace period before the owned Excel process tree is force-terminated. Every ordinary, snapshot, and `--no-build` test resolves its macro execution deadline from `--timeout-seconds`, then `commandDefaults.test.executionTimeoutSeconds`, then 600 seconds. Values must be positive, finite, whole seconds; zero, negative, fractional, and infinite values are invalid. This override does not alter build, workbook open/save, Host Event catalog, reference normalization, or cleanup deadlines. VS Code supplies no independent test timeout or shorter watchdog.

Workbook open and save stages each default to 300 seconds because their duration depends on source-template size and workbook contents. Projects may override these positive, finite, whole-second values through `commandDefaults.excelAutomation.workbookOpenTimeoutSeconds` and `commandDefaults.excelAutomation.workbookSaveTimeoutSeconds` in `vba-project.json`. The initial CLI does not expose per-invocation open or save timeout options; users may cancel an unexpectedly slow command with Ctrl+C, and one-off CLI overrides can be added later if a concrete need emerges.

`export` keeps exact-snapshot semantics: after success, the destination's importable VBA source and form sidecars match the source workbook, so modules removed from the workbook cannot silently reappear during a later `build`. Before mutating the **DocumentSourceSet** or an explicit destination, it exports every module to staging, computes and validates the complete placement and deletion plan, and copies every affected existing source file and sidecar to a recovery area on the destination file system. It then applies additions, replacements, and stale-source deletions without touching the source template or unrelated files. Any apply failure triggers rollback from the recovery area. A successful rollback removes the incomplete result and reports the original export failure; if rollback is incomplete, the command retains the recovery area and reports its path and manual recovery instructions. The recovery area is removed only after the exact snapshot has been committed successfully or rollback has been verified.

`import` performs an **ExplicitWorkbookImport** from a caller-provided source directory into a caller-provided workbook path without resolving project context. The closed **VbaSourceAdmission** intent `VbaSourceAdmissionIntent.ExplicitImport` fixes one recursive inventory of `.bas`, `.cls`, and `.frm` sources and their matching same-directory `.frx` sidecars. The text sources form a flat set ordered by extension-including exported file name. Relative paths are not ordering tie-breakers because duplicate exported source file names fail before Excel starts. Each selected source or sidecar is read once; an unreadable inventoried input fails admission without a rescan or retry. Sidecars remain opaque binary content consumed with their forms; orphan `.frx` files are ignored. Empty source sets and case-insensitive duplicate exported source file names also fail before Excel starts. The distinct closed `WorkbookMaterializationIntent.ExplicitImport` consumes those admitted artifacts without a second inventory, source read, or encoding decision. Unlike `build`, `import` uses the existing target as its workbook baseline, does not add, remove, or normalize **VbaProjectReferences** from a manifest, does not resolve CommonModules dependencies, does not interpret `'#ExcludePublish`, and does not validate whether the resulting workbook compiles.

Before Excel starts, `import` obtains authoritative `ModuleIdentity` and syntax facts from admission, rejects source-only conflicts, and derives the lossless ACP mirror. Before target staging, the materializer opens the existing target through a read-only `FileStream` with `FileShare.Read`. It holds that guard while copying the target into an invocation-owned workbook transaction, opening only that private copy in its owned **AutomationExcelProcess**, and inspecting the actual `VBProject.Name`, every active `Reference.Name`, and every component name that the flush will retain, including document modules. All source-to-source and source-to-target comparisons are case-insensitive. Invalid or incomplete source identity metadata, incomplete target inspection, or any conflict fails before component mutation. The target's current active reference set remains authoritative: `import` neither adds, removes, nor normalizes references. After preflight, the private copy's existing standard modules, class modules, and forms are flushed, while document modules remain. Imported component names, kinds, and projected code are verified against the same admitted facts before only the private copy is saved. Captured form sidecars are staged exactly from admitted bytes; sidecar-backed state remains covered by the established real-Excel semantic fixture rather than an exhaustive per-command runtime proof. After mirror cleanup and owned Excel-process release, the materializer validates the saved staging workbook, observes the final cancellation fence, releases the target guard, and attempts the synchronous atomic replacement once. Any earlier failure or cancellation preserves the original target bytes. The workflow does not reinspect source, compare-and-swap the target, retry, or roll back competing external changes, and the guard does not protect the final release-to-commit gap or non-cooperating changes. Incomplete cleanup reports the failure and retained private artifact paths for recovery. This compatibility and persistence contract does not claim that the resulting project compiles.

`test` follows `dotnet test` by building before it runs tests by default. `--no-build` skips that build and uses the existing bin workbook. It emits `text` or `ndjson`, with `text` as the default for command-line use. VS Code and other machine consumers should request `--format ndjson` explicitly. A complete schema `1.2` `runFinished` is authoritative for distinguishing workbook-owned test outcomes from infrastructure failure: a passed run exits zero, a run containing failed or error outcomes exits nonzero with the complete terminal record, and a nonzero exit without that record is a command-level failure even if partial test events exist. `testFinished.location` remains an optional object with its existing persistent URI and zero-based UTF-16 range shape. `test --no-build` does not add or update **VbaProjectReferences** in an existing bin workbook; the default build step applies manifest-defined references before tests run.

The ordinary and source-snapshot test paths inherit every module/project/library
name-conflict preflight from their build stage. `test --no-build` does not run
that source or manifest preflight because it executes the existing bin workbook
without materializing current source or references. A no-build success therefore
asserts only that the selected existing artifact ran successfully, not that the
current `DocumentSourceSet` can build. Failure to open or execute that workbook
remains ordinary test infrastructure failure, while `doctor` owns current source
health independently.

`test --source-snapshot <snapshot-directory>` instead fixes the same caller-owned complete **BuildSourceSnapshot** used by snapshot-aware `build`, creates an internal **SnapshotTestExecutionWorkspace**, and materializes a workbook there with the manifest-defined bin workbook's file name through the same closed `SourceSnapshotBuild` intent. Test execution opens the exact committed artifact returned by that intent rather than invoking a second build, runs the selected tests, releases every owned Excel process, and removes the workspace. It does not accept `--output`, delete the caller's snapshot directory, or read, open, create, replace, or delete the manifest-defined bin workbook path. `--source-snapshot` and `--no-build` are mutually exclusive. Success, failed assertions, command failure, and cancellation all use the same cleanup boundary. Failure to prove release of an owned Excel process is a command-level infrastructure error and does not become an individual test failure. After process release is proved, workspace deletion receives bounded retries; a remaining deletion failure reports the retained absolute path as a warning while preserving every workbook-owned test outcome and the exit status determined by those outcomes. For `--format ndjson`, the initial schema `1.2` implementation holds the event batch until process release succeeds. A process-release failure emits no event batch or `runFinished`, while a post-release deletion warning still emits the complete batch. `runFinished` therefore proves test execution and mandatory process ownership cleanup, not deletion of every temporary file. True event streaming remains deferred to issue #155.

Successful ordinary and snapshot build-before-test materialization internally pairs the exact committed workbook path with the **VbaSourceAdmission** that produced it. Before test execution, `VbaDev` copies the admitted module identities, callable declaration-name ranges, and safely mapped persistent source URIs into an immutable **ExecutedSourceIndex**. That index is the sole authority for locations emitted by the run and retains no path-backed content authority. Location resolution performs no source inventory, read, existence check, encoding detection, decode, or parse, so later edits, replacements, and deletions cannot change the locations associated with the workbook that ran.

For snapshot test results, procedure ranges come from the invocation-fixed snapshot admission, while each optional location URI identifies the corresponding persistent **DocumentSourceSet** path derived from preserved relative provenance. Ordinary built-test ranges and URIs come from the saved-source admission that materialized the bin workbook. Internal workspace paths never appear in result output. Unsafe, missing, or ambiguous module, procedure, or provenance mappings omit only the optional location, retain the workbook-owned identity and outcome, and emit a deterministic non-failing built-run warning.

`test --no-build` has no proved source capture for the existing bin workbook. It constructs no **ExecutedSourceIndex**, performs no project-source inspection for navigation, and always omits every optional source location regardless of current source state. Each completed no-build invocation emits exactly one fixed non-failing warning: `Warning: Source locations were omitted because --no-build runs an existing workbook without a proved source capture.` A usage or infrastructure failure before a completed test-result run does not add this warning. No implicit build, snapshot, save, retry, or editor coordination is introduced.

**ExecutedSourceIndex**, its admission pairing, and test-result location resolution are wholly owned by `VbaDev`; they add no dependency on `VscodeExtension`, Test Explorer, the language server, or the debug adapter. The BOM-or-fixed-ACP source-admission policy is unchanged.

`publish` does not require tests to pass; callers can compose `build`, `test`, and `publish` explicitly.

`export` has a project-aware mode and an explicit-workbook mode. When `--from` is omitted, `export` reads the manifest-resolved bin workbook and writes to the manifest-resolved document source set unless `--to` points at another directory. This project-aware mode requires project and document context. When `--from` is provided, `export` performs an **ExplicitWorkbookExport** from the caller-provided workbook path without resolving project context; if `--to` is omitted in this mode, the current working directory is the destination. `--project` and `--document` are rejected with `--from` because project context does not affect an **ExplicitWorkbookExport**. Export cleans the destination when the destination is manifest-owned or when `--to` is specified. When neither condition applies, export writes into the default destination without deleting unrelated existing files, but exported file paths that collide with existing files are overwritten. Cleanup-enabled exports record existing exported source relative paths, export the workbook to a temporary directory first, and only after that export succeeds recursively delete existing `.bas`, `.cls`, `.frm`, and related `.frx` files from the destination. Cleanup deletes files only and does not remove empty directories. If the workbook export fails, the destination is left untouched. Cleanup-enabled exports restore matching exported file names to their previous relative paths after export and write newly exported file names to the destination root. Stale, displaced, or duplicate `.frx` files in a cleanup-enabled destination do not fail export; they are deleted during cleanup, and exported forms write at most one matching `.frx` beside each `.frm`. Non-cleanup exports still write each exported `.frx` beside its `.frm`, but they do not delete displaced `.frx` files elsewhere in the destination. For exported forms, the `.frm` relative path is the form source unit location; the matching `.frx` is restored beside the `.frm` rather than as an independently placed file.

`vba-dev export` is non-interactive. Supplying the command and its project or
explicit destination is consent to the documented overwrite and cleanup
semantics, so the CLI does not prompt and remains suitable for automation. A
graphical caller may require confirmation before invoking a cleanup-enabled
export, but cancellation at that layer must occur before the CLI process starts
and does not change the command contract.

## Consequences

The command model favors predictable project automation over ad hoc workbook manipulation. It also commits the project manifest encoding, source layout, and manifest-resolved output paths early, which reduces ambiguity for future tools but requires explicit encoding handling in the C# implementation.

Excel and VBIDE automation remain a hard runtime dependency for workbook-backed commands. Environment problems should be surfaced through `doctor` and through clear command failures rather than hidden recovery behavior.
